EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 11 of 60+: FASHION VIDEOGRAPHER (Vidéaste de Mode / Réalisateur Mode)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Fashion Videographer role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Fashion Videographer represents a rapidly evolving and increasingly essential creative profession at the heart of modern fashion communication. According to industry research, fashion videographers are responsible for creating video content that showcases clothing, accessories, and runway collections for various platforms, including social media, e-commerce, and digital marketing campaigns . The role sits at the intersection of art, commerce, and technology, requiring mastery of cinematography, editing, and visual storytelling.


Behind the glamour lies a highly competitive field that demands technical mastery, creative vision, and the ability to adapt to rapidly changing platform requirements. The explosion of short-form video on platforms like TikTok and Instagram Reels has fundamentally transformed the role, with 2026 job descriptions emphasizing the ability to create "social-first video content" as a primary requirement . Fashion videographers must now be fluent in creating content optimized for multiple channels, from high-production-value campaign films to authentic behind-the-scenes content for social media.


The role encompasses a wide range of competencies, from concept development and shoot production to editing, color grading, and sound design . This multidisciplinary nature makes fashion videography one of the most demanding and rewarding roles in the creative industry, with leading brands offering base salaries ranging from $70,000 to $110,000 for experienced professionals .


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Vidéaste de Mode / Réalisateur Mode / Directeur de la Photographie
	English Job Title
	Fashion Videographer / Fashion Video Producer / Motion Content Creator
	ROME Code
	L1504 - Réalisation de films et de programmes audiovisuels; E1201 - Photographie
	Convention Collective
	Audiovisuel - IDCC 3043; Presse Spécialisée Mode
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Visual Content
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Video Lead / Head of Content / Creative Producer
	Supervises
	Camera Assistants, Lighting Technicians, Production Assistants
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Videographer operates at the intersection of art, commerce, and technology. According to industry job descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions :


* Creative Direction: Developing concepts that align with brand aesthetics and editorial visions
* Technical Production: Setting up professional camera systems, lighting, and audio equipment
* Subject Direction: Coaching talent, directing models, and managing on-set talent relationships
* Post-Production: Editing footage, color grading, sound mixing, and delivering finished content
* Platform Optimization: Creating content optimized for specific platforms (YouTube, Instagram, TikTok, etc.)
* Collaboration: Working with stylists, models, producers, editors, and social media teams


The role has been fundamentally transformed by the rise of short-form video, with 2026 job descriptions emphasizing the ability to create "highly engaging, short-form content" across multiple platforms . Fashion videographers must now be "social-first" in their approach, understanding platform nuances and emerging content formats.
1.3 Key Synonyms
Synonym
	Description
	Vidéaste de Mode
	Fashion Videographer
	Réalisateur Mode
	Fashion Director / Video Producer
	Motion Content Creator
	Content Creator specializing in video
	Directeur de la Photographie
	Director of Photography
	Supervising Producer
	Senior Video Producer overseeing production teams
	Creative Producer

Level

According to job postings, a Bachelors or Associates degree with a focus in Audio and Video Production is preferred, but significant professional experience (5+ years) is equally valued . Many successful fashion videographers have followed a practical pathway, building portfolios through assisting and freelance work rather than formal education .

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview
Position Title: Fashion Videographer (Vidéaste de Mode)


Department: Creative / Production / Content


Reports To: Creative Director / Video Lead / Head of Content


Location: Paris / London / Milan / New York (with travel)


Employment Type: Freelance / Full-time / Agency-represented
4.2 Purpose of the Role
To create high-quality, engaging video content that showcases fashion products, tells brand stories, and drives audience engagement across multiple platforms. According to leading fashion brands, the role is responsible for "producing high-quality, end-to-end video content that supports marketing, social, and e-commerce initiatives" . The Fashion Videographer translates creative briefs into compelling visual stories, maintaining a strong visual and storytelling standard that reflects the brand's identity .
4.3 Key Responsibilities

	Post-Production
	Edit video content using industry-standard tools, including basic color grading and sound design

	Per project

SECTION 5: ON-THE-ROLE DAILY DUTIES
5.1 Daily Schedule
Time
	Activity
	Description
	08:00 - 09:00
	Preparation
	Review shot lists, check equipment, prepare for shoot
	09:00 - 12:00
	Production
	Film content, direct talent, manage lighting and audio
	12:00 - 13:00
	Lunch Review
	Review footage, plan afternoon shots
	13:00 - 16:00
	Production Continuation
	Continue filming, capture b-roll, additional angles
	16:00 - 18:00
	Wrap & Backup
	Backup footage, upload for post-production
	18:00 - 22:00+
	Post-Production
	Editing, color grading, sound design, content delivery
	5.2 Weekly Activities

	Description

	Post-Production
	1-2 days/week
	Editing, color grading, sound design
	Team Meetings
	Weekly
	Creative alignment, project reviews
	Trend Research
	Ongoing
	Monitoring emerging trends and formats
	5.3 Key Responsibilities According to Industry Standards
Activity
	Description
	Set Preparation
	Set up video set including camera, audio, lighting, and fixtures
	Technical Execution
	Ensure proper lighting, focus, and composition for videos and b-roll
	Talent Direction
	Coach talent on set to create engaging content
	Audio Management
	Manage audio and audit content while filming
	Asset Management
	Backup footage, upload for post-production, maintain organized file systems
	Equipment Maintenance
	Maintain audio and video supplies and equipment

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 11 (Revised): FASHION VIDEOGRAPHER (Vidéaste de Mode / Réalisateur Mode / Directeur de la Photographie)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Fashion Videographer role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Fashion Videographer represents a rapidly evolving and increasingly essential creative profession at the heart of modern fashion communication. According to industry job descriptions, fashion videographers are responsible for creating video content that showcases clothing, accessories, and runway collections for various platforms, including social media, e-commerce, and digital marketing campaigns. The role sits at the intersection of art, commerce, and technology, requiring mastery of cinematography, editing, and visual storytelling.


The explosion of short-form video on platforms like TikTok and Instagram Reels has fundamentally transformed the role, with 2026 job descriptions emphasizing the ability to create "social-first video content" as a primary requirement. Fashion videographers must now be fluent in creating content optimized for multiple channels, from high-production-value campaign films to authentic behind-the-scenes content for social media. According to industry research, video content now accounts for over 80% of all internet traffic, with fashion brands increasingly prioritizing video over static imagery.


The profession encompasses a wide range of specializations, from high-production campaign films and fashion documentaries to social media content, product videos, and behind-the-scenes coverage. According to industry sources, the most successful fashion videographers are those who have developed a distinctive visual style while mastering the technical demands of multiple platforms. The role is evolving rapidly with the adoption of digital technologies, with AI-assisted editing, virtual production, and real-time effects becoming increasingly important.

	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Videographer operates at the intersection of art, commerce, and technology. According to industry job descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Creative Direction: Developing concepts that align with brand aesthetics and editorial visions
* Technical Production: Setting up professional camera systems, lighting, and audio equipment
* Subject Direction: Coaching talent, directing models, and managing on-set talent relationships
* Post-Production: Editing footage, color grading, sound mixing, and delivering finished content
* Platform Optimization: Creating content optimized for specific platforms (YouTube, Instagram, TikTok, etc.)
* Collaboration: Working with stylists, models, producers, editors, and social media teams
* Trend Monitoring: Staying current on video trends and applying best practices


The role has been fundamentally transformed by the rise of short-form video, with 2026 job descriptions emphasizing the ability to create "highly engaging, short-form content" across multiple platforms. Fashion videographers must now be "social-first" in their approach, understanding platform nuances and emerging content formats. According to industry data, short-form video platforms like TikTok and Instagram Reels now drive more engagement for fashion brands than traditional video formats.


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements
	Campaign Video
	High-production brand campaigns
	Cinematic quality, storytelling, client management
	Social Media Video
	Short-form content for social platforms
	Platform expertise, speed, trends awareness
	Runway Video
	Fashion show coverage, runway recaps
	Speed, accuracy, show access, quick turnaround
	Documentary/Fashion Film
	Brand storytelling, behind-the-scenes
	Narrative skills, long-form content, authenticity
	Product/E-commerce Video
	Product showcases, e-commerce content
	Consistency, technical precision, volume
	Beauty Video
	Beauty campaigns, tutorials
	Lighting mastery, close-up work, beauty industry knowledge
	Music Video
	Fashion/music crossovers
	Creative vision, music industry connections
	Live Streaming
	Live coverage of fashion events
	Technical expertise, real-time production

4.2 Purpose of the Role
To create high-quality, engaging video content that showcases fashion products, tells brand stories, and drives audience engagement across multiple platforms. According to leading fashion brands, the role is responsible for "producing high-quality, end-to-end video content that supports marketing, social, and e-commerce initiatives." The Fashion Videographer translates creative briefs into compelling visual stories, maintaining a strong visual and storytelling standard that reflects the brand's identity.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 12 of 60+: FASHION STYLIST (Styliste de Mode / Personal Stylist)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Fashion Stylist role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Fashion Stylist is a multifaceted creative professional who curates and orchestrates complete looks, working at the intersection of fashion, art, and commerce. According to the ONISEP definition, the stylist "imagines the models for upcoming collections in ready-to-wear or luxury. Clothing, accessories, leather goods... they know how to anticipate trends, but also adapt to budget and production constraints" . This role requires a unique combination of creative vision, technical knowledge of garment construction and textiles, and a deep understanding of consumer behavior and market trends.


The profession operates across two primary contexts: editorial/creative styling for magazines, advertising campaigns, and runway shows, and retail/personal styling for luxury boutiques and individual clients . According to the ONISEP, fashion stylists in France typically require a minimum of Bac+3 (Bachelor's degree), with starting salaries around €2,500 per month . In the US market, stylists in major metropolitan areas like New York earn average salaries of $61,642, with top earners in luxury segments exceeding $75,000 annually .


Recent industry research from Fashion UK (Bectu) reveals significant challenges facing fashion creatives, including stylists: only 14% are paid on time, 79% have felt pressured to work for free, and 83% report tolerating toxic workplace behaviors . These findings underscore the critical need for professional development, advocacy, and support systems within the industry—areas where EOEX is uniquely positioned to provide value.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Styliste de Mode / Styliste Personnelle / Styliste de Collection
	English Job Title
	Fashion Stylist / Personal Stylist / Editorial Stylist / Retail Stylist
	ROME Code
	B1701 - Stylisme - modélisme (textile)
	Convention Collective
	Couture Parisienne - IDCC 303; Presse Spécialisée Mode; Commerce de Détail - IDCC 1505
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Design & Product Development
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Editorial Director / Store Manager / Head of Styling
	Supervises
	Junior Stylists, Styling Assistants, Wardrobe Assistants, Interns
	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Stylist operates at a critical junction in the fashion ecosystem, serving as the creative bridge between designers, brands, and consumers. The role encompasses several key dimensions :


* Creative Vision: Defining and executing the visual identity of collections, editorials, and brand campaigns
* Trend Intelligence: Anticipating and interpreting fashion trends to create commercially viable looks
* Technical Expertise: Understanding garment construction, textiles, fit, and silhouette to create cohesive ensembles
* Client Relationship Management: Building and maintaining long-term relationships with individual and corporate clients
* Cross-Functional Collaboration: Working with designers, photographers, models, makeup artists, and production teams


The role is increasingly bifurcated between two distinct career paths:


Retail/Luxury Stylists: According to FENDI job descriptions, the In-Store Stylist "provides expert fashion guidance and personalized styling services, combining deep product knowledge with a strong understanding of fashion trends, body types, and individual lifestyles" . Key responsibilities include delivering one-to-one styling consultations, creating complete outfit recommendations, and building client portfolios through exceptional service and styling expertise .


Editorial/Creative Stylists: These professionals work on magazine editorials, advertising campaigns, and runway shows. They are responsible for conceptualizing and executing complete looks that tell a visual story, often working with top photographers, models, and art directors.

According to the ONISEP, the minimum access level for the stylist profession in France is Bac+3 . However, the most competitive positions, particularly in luxury houses and top editorial publications, often require a Bac+5 or equivalent professional experience . In contrast, salary surveys indicate that in the US, many fashion stylists enter the profession with a high school diploma, with experience and portfolio being the primary determinants of success .

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview (Luxury Retail Stylist)
Position Title: In-Store Stylist (Styliste en Boutique)


Department: Retail / Client Experience


Reports To: Store Manager


Location: Paris, France


Employment Type: Full-time (CDI)
4.2 Purpose of the Role (Based on FENDI Description)
According to FENDI, the In-Store Stylist "elevates styling standards, Ready-to-Wear performance, and client experience within the boutique" . The role "goes beyond traditional selling by creating complete silhouettes, strengthening long-term client relationships, and inspiring a strong fashion culture within the team" .


The Stylist is "the ultimate ambassador" for the maison, serving as a "trustworthy advisor to clients and a reliable team player that thrives to provide the best experience" .
4.3 Key Responsibilities
Category
	Responsibility
	Frequency
	Personal Styling
	Deliver one-to-one styling consultations, create complete outfit recommendations, provide expert advice on fit, color, proportions, and accessorizing
	Daily
	Client Relationship
	Build and actively manage personal client portfolio, strengthen client loyalty through follow-up, organize private styling sessions
	Ongoing
	Sales Performance
	Drive styling-related sales, UPT, conversion, and client appointments in line with store targets
	Daily
	Fashion Expertise
	Act as in-store reference for brand style direction, translate fashion trends into wearable suggestions
	Ongoing
	Team Development
	Train and coach Client Advisors on styling techniques, wardrobing, and full-look selling
	Weekly
	Operational Excellence
	Maintain impeccable store environment, support visual merchandising, manage client data and CRM tools
	Daily
	4.4 Position Overview (Editorial/Creative Stylist)

	Responsibility

	Source garments, accessories, and props from showrooms, designers, and archives

* Using extensive knowledge of the fashion industry

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 12 (Revised): FASHION STYLIST (Styliste de Mode / Personal Stylist / Styliste Personnelle)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Fashion Stylist role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Fashion Stylist is a multifaceted creative professional who curates and orchestrates complete looks, working at the intersection of fashion, art, and commerce. According to the ONISEP definition, the stylist "imagines the models for upcoming collections in ready-to-wear or luxury. Clothing, accessories, leather goods... they know how to anticipate trends, but also adapt to budget and production constraints." This role requires a unique combination of creative vision, technical knowledge of garment construction and textiles, and a deep understanding of consumer behavior and market trends.


The profession operates across two primary contexts: editorial/creative styling for magazines, advertising campaigns, and runway shows, and retail/personal styling for luxury boutiques and individual clients. According to the ONISEP, fashion stylists in France typically require a minimum of Bac+3 (Bachelor's degree), with starting salaries around €2,500 per month. In the US market, stylists in major metropolitan areas like New York earn average salaries of $61,642, with top earners in luxury segments exceeding $75,000 annually.


Recent industry research from Fashion UK (Bectu) reveals significant challenges facing fashion creatives, including stylists: only 14% are paid on time, 79% have felt pressured to work for free, and 83% report tolerating toxic workplace behaviors. These findings underscore the critical need for professional development, advocacy, and support systems within the industry—areas where EOEX is uniquely positioned to provide value. The profession encompasses various specializations, from editorial and commercial styling to personal shopping, wardrobe consulting, and celebrity styling, each requiring distinct skills and approaches.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Styliste de Mode / Styliste Personnelle / Styliste de Collection / Styliste de Presse
	English Job Title
	Fashion Stylist / Personal Stylist / Editorial Stylist / Retail Stylist
	ROME Code
	B1701 - Stylisme - modélisme (textile); L1502 - Costume et habillage spectacle
	Convention Collective
	Couture Parisienne - IDCC 303; Presse Spécialisée Mode - IDCC 303; Commerce de Détail - IDCC 1505
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Design & Product Development
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Editorial Director / Store Manager / Head of Styling
	Supervises
	Junior Stylists, Styling Assistants, Wardrobe Assistants, Interns
	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo / Sydney
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Stylist operates at a critical junction in the fashion ecosystem, serving as the creative bridge between designers, brands, and consumers. The role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Creative Vision: Defining and executing the visual identity of collections, editorials, and brand campaigns
* Trend Intelligence: Anticipating and interpreting fashion trends to create commercially viable looks
* Technical Expertise: Understanding garment construction, textiles, fit, and silhouette to create cohesive ensembles
* Client Relationship Management: Building and maintaining long-term relationships with individual and corporate clients
* Cross-Functional Collaboration: Working with designers, photographers, models, makeup artists, and production teams
* Business Acumen: Managing budgets, negotiating rates, and understanding commercial value


The role is increasingly bifurcated between two distinct career paths:


Retail/Luxury Stylists: According to FENDI job descriptions, the In-Store Stylist "provides expert fashion guidance and personalized styling services, combining deep product knowledge with a strong understanding of fashion trends, body types, and individual lifestyles." Key responsibilities include delivering one-to-one styling consultations, creating complete outfit recommendations, and building client portfolios through exceptional service and styling expertise.


Editorial/Creative Stylists: These professionals work on magazine editorials, advertising campaigns, and runway shows. They are responsible for conceptualizing and executing complete looks that tell a visual story, often working with top photographers, models, and art directors. This path requires exceptional creative vision, networking skills, and the ability to work under tight deadlines and budgets.

According to the ONISEP, the minimum access level for the stylist profession in France is Bac+3. However, the most competitive positions, particularly in luxury houses and top editorial publications, often require a Bac+5 or equivalent professional experience. In contrast, salary surveys indicate that in the US, many fashion stylists enter the profession with a high school diploma, with experience and portfolio being the primary determinants of success.

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview (Luxury Retail Stylist)
Position Title: In-Store Stylist (Styliste en Boutique)


Department: Retail / Client Experience


Reports To: Store Manager


Location: Paris, France


Employment Type: Full-time (CDI)
4.2 Purpose of the Role (Based on FENDI Description)
According to FENDI, the In-Store Stylist "elevates styling standards, Ready-to-Wear performance, and client experience within the boutique." The role "goes beyond traditional selling by creating complete silhouettes, strengthening long-term client relationships, and inspiring a strong fashion culture within the team."


The Stylist is "the ultimate ambassador" for the maison, serving as a "trustworthy advisor to clients and a reliable team player that thrives to provide the best experience."
4.3 Key Responsibilities (Retail/Luxury Stylist)

	Ongoing
	Sales Performance
	Drive styling-related sales, UPT, conversion, and client appointments in line with store targets
	Daily
	Fashion Expertise
	Act as in-store reference for brand style direction, translate fashion trends into wearable suggestions

	Train and coach Client Advisors on styling techniques, wardrobing, and full-look selling

	Daily

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 13 of 60+: MAKEUP ARTIST (Maquilleur / Maquilleuse de Mode)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Makeup Artist role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Makeup Artist is an essential creative professional at the heart of fashion production, responsible for translating creative visions into reality through the artistry of makeup and beauty. According to industry descriptions, fashion and editorial makeup "occupies a different creative register to screen and stage work, with a greater emphasis on visual concept, trend literacy, and collaboration with photographers, creative directors, and stylists" . Artists in this sector work across fashion week runways, beauty campaigns, magazine editorials, and celebrity red carpet events .


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) of over 500 fashion creatives revealed that "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do" . A staggering "eight in 10 (79%) have felt pressured to work for free," while "83% say that behaviours that would be considered toxic and inappropriate in public life are often tolerated in the fashion industry" . Nearly a third of respondents (31%) "don't see themselves in the industry in five years' time" .


The makeup artistry profession spans diverse specializations, from editorial and fashion week work to celebrity red carpet styling, beauty campaigns, and commercial work. As noted by industry experts, "Many artists enter this sector by assisting established professionals and building a portfolio through commercial shoots, test days, and smaller editorial projects before progressing to lead roles on campaigns and runway shows" .


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Maquilleur / Maquilleuse de Mode / Maquilleur Artistique
	English Job Title
	Makeup Artist / Fashion Makeup Artist / Editorial Makeup Artist
	ROME Code
	B1701 - Stylisme - modélisme (textile); L1502 - Costume et habillage spectacle
	Convention Collective
	Presse Spécialisée Mode; Spectacle - IDCC 3090
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Beauty & Fashion
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Photo Director / Head of Makeup / Stylist
	Supervises
	Makeup Assistants, Junior Makeup Artists, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Makeup Artist operates across multiple creative domains, each with distinct requirements and expectations:


Editorial Makeup Artistry: Artists working on magazine editorials, beauty campaigns, and creative fashion features. According to the Iver Makeup Academy, artists in this area must demonstrate "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently," which is "as important as technical skill" . The range of looks required is broad, from high-fashion conceptual work to natural beauty and avant-garde creations.


Fashion Week & Runway Makeup: Runway work requires artists to work under extreme time pressure, often creating looks for 20-50 models within a few hours. Fashion week work involves "team coordination, last-minute curveballs, and navigating the industry's financial grey areas" . Andrea Damyon, a makeup artist who worked on multiple Fashion Weeks, emphasizes that "genuine connection is your best career currency" in this environment .


Retail & Luxury Makeup Artistry: According to CHANEL job descriptions, a Makeup Artist plays a key role in "elevating the makeup category through immersive consultations, storytelling, and service-led experiences that connect clients deeply with our brand" . They "lead by example in delivering a best-in-class client experience, support business growth through innovative events and masterclasses, and act as a mentor and ambassador to colleagues" . These roles require a minimum of 2 years of experience .


Celebrity & Red Carpet: The pinnacle of fashion makeup artistry, working with high-profile celebrities for red carpet events. According to Gucci Westman, the legendary celebrity makeup artist, success in this area requires "understanding the assignment" and adapting "depending on who I'm working with, the objective may change" . Westman emphasizes that versatility, focus, and being "attuned to clientele" are essential: "I always try to do something slightly different, but I never want to create some show pony for makeup" .


Commercial & Advertising Makeup: Working on advertising campaigns, commercial shoots, and e-commerce content. This requires consistency, speed, and the ability to create looks that photograph well across multiple angles and lighting conditions.
1.3 Key Synonyms
Synonym
	Description
	Maquilleur de Mode
	Fashion Makeup Artist
	Maquilleur Artistique
	Artistic Makeup Artist
	Maquilleur de Presse
	Press/Editorial Makeup Artist
	Maquilleur de Défilé
	Runway Makeup Artist
	Maquilleur de Célébrités
	Celebrity Makeup Artist
	Maquilleur de Studio
	Studio Makeup Artist

SECTION 2: EDUCATIONAL REQUIREMENTS & CERTIFICATIONS
2.1 Formal Education Requirements
Level
	Requirement
	Duration
	Institutions
	Minimum
	Diploma in Makeup Artistry
	1-2 years
	Various accredited institutions
	Preferred
	Advanced Fashion Makeup Course
	1-2 years
	Iver Makeup Academy, Carlow Institute
	Alternative
	Professional experience pathway
	Varies
	Assisting, self-teaching
	

Makeup artistry education typically involves a combination of hands-on training, theory, and practical application. According to the Iver Makeup Academy's approach, graduates secure "their first professional credits within weeks of completing their course, through the academy agency and through the professional network built during their time" in training .


Specialized courses cover:


* Advanced high-fashion makeup
* Makeup for theatre, TV, and film
* Special effects makeup
* Airbrush makeup
* Fashion and photographic makeup
* Creative hair
2.2 Alternative Entry Pathways
The makeup artistry profession is notable for allowing alternative entry pathways. According to the podcast interview with Lottie, an LA-based makeup artist, she transitioned "from an accounting career into the beauty industry without any formal training," teaching herself "through magazines and hands-on experience" before being "discovered by Pat McGrath on MySpace" . This demonstrates that while formal education provides a structured foundation, exceptional talent, perseverance, and networking can also open doors.


Similarly, Gucci Westman's journey began with "loving painting as a child" and attending "makeup school in Paris, as well as special effects courses in Los Angeles" before cracking "into the editorial world" through a job with Annie Leibovitz for Vanity Fair . Westman's apprenticeship with masters helped her build a career that now spans "red carpet beauty, dozens of editorials and even a blockbuster namesake brand" .

	Techniques

	Annual
	Business Skills
	Pricing, contracts, client management
	Annual
	Digital Skills
	Social media, digital portfolio, online presence
	Ongoing

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 14 of 60+: HAIR STYLIST (Coiffeur / Coiffeuse de Mode / Hair Stylist)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Hair Stylist role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Hair Stylist is an essential creative professional at the heart of fashion production, responsible for translating creative visions into reality through the artistry of hair design. According to the National Careers Service, hair stylists "advise clients on haircare and hairstyles, shampoo and condition hair, cut, colour and style hair, sometimes for fashion shows, film, TV and theatre productions" . In the fashion industry, hair stylists work across fashion week runways, editorial shoots, advertising campaigns, celebrity red carpet events, and e-commerce productions, working in close collaboration with creative directors, photographers, stylists, and makeup artists.


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding in recent years. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) of over 500 fashion creatives revealed significant challenges facing all fashion professionals, including hair stylists: "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do" . A staggering "eight in 10 (79%) have felt pressured to work for free," while "83% say that behaviours that would be considered toxic and inappropriate in public life are often tolerated in the fashion industry" . The survey also found that "69% of respondents are not paid for overtime, with 24% working 11-hour days or more" and "51% earn less than £30,000 a year" . Nearly a third of respondents (31%) "don't see themselves in the industry in five years' time."


The profession requires a unique combination of technical expertise, creative vision, and exceptional collaboration skills. Hair stylists must master diverse techniques including cutting, colouring, styling, and wig work, while staying current with rapidly evolving trends. As noted by industry experts, "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently is just as important as technical skill" . The profession also demands significant business acumen, as many hair stylists work freelance, requiring them to manage their own finances, marketing, and client relationships.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Coiffeur / Coiffeuse de Mode / Hair Stylist / Coiffeur Artistique
	English Job Title
	Hair Stylist / Fashion Hair Stylist / Editorial Hair Stylist / Session Hairstylist
	ROME Code
	D1205 - Coiffure; L1502 - Costume et habillage spectacle
	Convention Collective
	Coiffure - IDCC 2856; Presse Spécialisée Mode; Spectacle - IDCC 3090
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Beauty & Fashion
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Photo Director / Head of Hair / Stylist
	Supervises
	Hair Assistants, Junior Hair Stylists, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles / Sydney
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Hair Stylist operates across multiple creative domains, each with distinct requirements and expectations:


Editorial Hair Styling: Artists working on magazine editorials, beauty campaigns, and creative fashion features. This work involves creating looks for high-fashion editorials, often requiring conceptual and avant-garde approaches that push creative boundaries. According to job descriptions, editorial hair stylists must demonstrate "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently," which is "as important as technical skill" .


Fashion Week & Runway Hair: Runway work requires hair stylists to work under extreme time pressure, often creating looks for 20-50 models within a few hours. Fashion week work involves "team coordination, last-minute curveballs, and navigating the industry's financial grey areas" . According to industry professionals, "genuine connection is your best career currency" in this high-pressure environment .


Celebrity & Red Carpet Hair: The pinnacle of fashion hair styling, working with high-profile celebrities for red carpet events and premieres. This requires versatility, discretion, and the ability to create looks that are both camera-ready and red-carpet appropriate. As noted by leading celebrity hairstylists, success in this area requires understanding "the assignment" and adapting to "the objective" .


Commercial & Advertising Hair: Working on advertising campaigns, commercial shoots, and e-commerce content. This requires consistency, speed, and the ability to create looks that photograph well across multiple angles and lighting conditions.


Retail & Salon Work: Many hair stylists maintain salon work as a foundation for their fashion career, providing steady income while building their fashion portfolio. According to industry research, some stylists maintain salon positions or part-time roles to support their fashion work.

The hair styling profession allows for alternative entry pathways. According to industry professionals, many successful fashion hair stylists:

* Attend Fashion Weeks: Network and gain experience

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 13 (Revised): MAKEUP ARTIST (Maquilleur / Maquilleuse de Mode / Maquilleur Artistique)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Makeup Artist role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Makeup Artist is an essential creative professional at the heart of fashion production, responsible for translating creative visions into reality through the artistry of makeup and beauty. According to industry descriptions, fashion and editorial makeup "occupies a different creative register to screen and stage work, with a greater emphasis on visual concept, trend literacy, and collaboration with photographers, creative directors, and stylists." Artists in this sector work across fashion week runways, beauty campaigns, magazine editorials, and celebrity red carpet events.


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) of over 500 fashion creatives revealed that "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do." A staggering "eight in 10 (79%) have felt pressured to work for free," while "83% say that behaviours that would be considered toxic and inappropriate in public life are often tolerated in the fashion industry." Nearly a third of respondents (31%) "don't see themselves in the industry in five years' time."


The makeup artistry profession spans diverse specializations, from editorial and fashion week work to celebrity red carpet styling, beauty campaigns, and commercial work. As noted by industry experts, "Many artists enter this sector by assisting established professionals and building a portfolio through commercial shoots, test days, and smaller editorial projects before progressing to lead roles on campaigns and runway shows." The role is evolving with the integration of AI tools, virtual try-on technologies, and the growing demand for inclusive beauty representation.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Maquilleur / Maquilleuse de Mode / Maquilleur Artistique / Maquilleur de Presse
	English Job Title
	Makeup Artist / Fashion Makeup Artist / Editorial Makeup Artist / Beauty Artist
	ROME Code
	B1701 - Stylisme - modélisme (textile); L1502 - Costume et habillage spectacle
	Convention Collective
	Presse Spécialisée Mode - IDCC 303; Spectacle - IDCC 3090
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Beauty & Fashion
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Photo Director / Head of Makeup / Stylist
	Supervises
	Makeup Assistants, Junior Makeup Artists, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Makeup Artist operates across multiple creative domains, each with distinct requirements and expectations:


Editorial Makeup Artistry: Artists working on magazine editorials, beauty campaigns, and creative fashion features. According to the Iver Makeup Academy, artists in this area must demonstrate "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently," which is "as important as technical skill." The range of looks required is broad, from high-fashion conceptual work to natural beauty and avant-garde creations.


Fashion Week & Runway Makeup: Runway work requires artists to work under extreme time pressure, often creating looks for 20-50 models within a few hours. Fashion week work involves "team coordination, last-minute curveballs, and navigating the industry's financial grey areas." Andrea Damyon, a makeup artist who worked on multiple Fashion Weeks, emphasizes that "genuine connection is your best career currency" in this environment.


Retail & Luxury Makeup Artistry: According to CHANEL job descriptions, a Makeup Artist plays a key role in "elevating the makeup category through immersive consultations, storytelling, and service-led experiences that connect clients deeply with our brand." They "lead by example in delivering a best-in-class client experience, support business growth through innovative events and masterclasses, and act as a mentor and ambassador to colleagues."


Celebrity & Red Carpet: The pinnacle of fashion makeup artistry, working with high-profile celebrities for red carpet events. According to Gucci Westman, the legendary celebrity makeup artist, success in this area requires "understanding the assignment" and adapting "depending on who I'm working with, the objective may change." Westman emphasizes that versatility, focus, and being "attuned to clientele" are essential.


Commercial & Advertising Makeup: Working on advertising campaigns, commercial shoots, and e-commerce content. This requires consistency, speed, and the ability to create looks that photograph well across multiple angles and lighting conditions.

Makeup artistry education typically involves a combination of hands-on training, theory, and practical application. According to the Iver Makeup Academy's approach, graduates secure "their first professional credits within weeks of completing their course, through the academy agency and through the professional network built during their time" in training.
2.2 Specialized Course Coverage

	Fashion and Photographic Makeup

	Creative Hair
	Styling for fashion and editorial
	2.3 Alternative Entry Pathways
The makeup artistry profession is notable for allowing alternative entry pathways. According to the podcast interview with Lottie, an LA-based makeup artist, she transitioned "from an accounting career into the beauty industry without any formal training," teaching herself "through magazines and hands-on experience" before being "discovered by Pat McGrath on MySpace." This demonstrates that while formal education provides a structured foundation, exceptional talent, perseverance, and networking can also open doors.


Similarly, Gucci Westman's journey began with "loving painting as a child" and attending "makeup school in Paris, as well as special effects courses in Los Angeles" before cracking "into the editorial world" through a job with Annie Leibovitz for Vanity Fair. Westman's apprenticeship with masters helped her build a career that now spans "red carpet beauty, dozens of editorials and even a blockbuster namesake brand."

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 14 (Revised): HAIR STYLIST (Coiffeur / Coiffeuse de Mode / Hair Stylist)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Hair Stylist role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Hair Stylist is an essential creative professional at the heart of fashion production, responsible for translating creative visions into reality through the artistry of hair design. According to the National Careers Service, hair stylists "advise clients on haircare and hairstyles, shampoo and condition hair, cut, colour and style hair, sometimes for fashion shows, film, TV and theatre productions." In the fashion industry, hair stylists work across fashion week runways, editorial shoots, advertising campaigns, celebrity red carpet events, and e-commerce productions, working in close collaboration with creative directors, photographers, stylists, and makeup artists.


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding in recent years. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) of over 500 fashion creatives revealed significant challenges facing all fashion professionals, including hair stylists: "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do." A staggering "eight in 10 (79%) have felt pressured to work for free," while "83% say that behaviours that would be considered toxic and inappropriate in public life are often tolerated in the fashion industry." The survey also found that "69% of respondents are not paid for overtime, with 24% working 11-hour days or more" and "51% earn less than £30,000 a year." Nearly a third of respondents (31%) "don't see themselves in the industry in five years' time."


The profession requires a unique combination of technical expertise, creative vision, and exceptional collaboration skills. Hair stylists must master diverse techniques including cutting, colouring, styling, and wig work, while staying current with rapidly evolving trends. As noted by industry experts, "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently is just as important as technical skill." The profession also demands significant business acumen, as many hair stylists work freelance, requiring them to manage their own finances, marketing, and client relationships. The rise of social media has also transformed the profession, with platforms like Instagram becoming essential for portfolio presentation and client acquisition.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Coiffeur / Coiffeuse de Mode / Hair Stylist / Coiffeur Artistique / Chef Coiffeur
	English Job Title
	Hair Stylist / Fashion Hair Stylist / Editorial Hair Stylist / Session Hairstylist / Runway Hairstylist
	ROME Code
	D1205 - Coiffure; L1502 - Costume et habillage spectacle
	Convention Collective
	Coiffure - IDCC 2856; Presse Spécialisée Mode - IDCC 303; Spectacle - IDCC 3090
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Beauty & Fashion
	Reports To
	Creative Director / Photo Director / Head of Hair / Stylist
	Supervises
	Hair Assistants, Junior Hair Stylists, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles / Sydney / Tokyo
	1.2 Professional Context
The Fashion Hair Stylist operates across multiple creative domains, each with distinct requirements and expectations:


Editorial Hair Styling: Artists working on magazine editorials, beauty campaigns, and creative fashion features. This work involves creating looks for high-fashion editorials, often requiring conceptual and avant-garde approaches that push creative boundaries. According to job descriptions, editorial hair stylists must demonstrate "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently," which is "as important as technical skill." The range of looks required is broad, from high-fashion conceptual work to natural textures and avant-garde creations.


Fashion Week & Runway Hair: Runway work requires hair stylists to work under extreme time pressure, often creating looks for 20-50 models within a few hours. Fashion week work involves "team coordination, last-minute curveballs, and navigating the industry's financial grey areas." According to industry professionals, "genuine connection is your best career currency" in this high-pressure environment. Hair stylists must be able to create consistent, polished looks quickly while managing multiple models simultaneously.


Celebrity & Red Carpet Hair: The pinnacle of fashion hair styling, working with high-profile celebrities for red carpet events and premieres. This requires versatility, discretion, and the ability to create looks that are both camera-ready and red-carpet appropriate. As noted by leading celebrity hairstylists, success in this area requires understanding "the assignment" and adapting to "the objective." Celebrity hair stylists must also navigate the demands of high-profile clients and the pressures of public appearances.


Commercial & Advertising Hair: Working on advertising campaigns, commercial shoots, and e-commerce content. This requires consistency, speed, and the ability to create looks that photograph well across multiple angles and lighting conditions. Commercial hair stylists must work within tight budgets and timelines while delivering high-quality results.


Retail & Salon Work: Many hair stylists maintain salon work as a foundation for their fashion career, providing steady income while building their fashion portfolio. According to industry research, some stylists maintain salon positions or part-time roles to support their fashion work. This dual career path allows stylists to develop their skills while maintaining financial stability.

	2.4 Alternative Entry Pathways
The hair styling profession allows for alternative entry pathways. According to industry professionals, many successful fashion hair stylists:


* Start in Salons: Build foundational skills in commercial salons
* Assist Established Stylists: Learn on the job while building portfolio
* Attend Fashion Weeks: Network and gain experience
* Build Portfolio through Test Shoots: Create editorial-quality images
* Self-Teaching: Learn through practice, observation, and continuous learning

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 15 of 60+: MODEL AGENT (Agent de Mannequins / Talent Agent)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Model Agent role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Model Agent is a pivotal figure in the fashion ecosystem, serving as the critical bridge between talent and opportunity. According to industry job descriptions, a Model Agent "plays a pivotal role in discovering, developing and managing the careers of fashion models" while "building meaningful relationships with both talent and clients" . The role encompasses the full spectrum of talent management, from scouting and development to booking, negotiation, and career strategy.


The profession operates within a dynamic and often challenging industry context. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) revealed that "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do" . This environment demands that Model Agents not only possess exceptional commercial acumen and industry knowledge but also demonstrate genuine commitment to talent welfare and career development. As noted in industry descriptions, successful agents are "passionate about helping others achieve long-term career success" while navigating the "dynamic, fast-paced role requiring exceptional organizational skills, commercial acumen, creativity and a genuine passion for fashion" .


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Agent de Mannequins / Agent Artistique / Talent Agent
	English Job Title
	Model Agent / Talent Agent / Booking Agent
	ROME Code
	M1602 - Gestion de portefeuille de clients; K1801 - Conseil en relations publiques
	Convention Collective
	Agences de Mannequins - IDCC 303 (Couture Parisienne); Artistes - IDCC 3090
	Job Family
	Talent Management / Agency Operations
	Reports To
	Agency Director / Head of Talent / Managing Director
	Supervises
	Junior Agents, Talent Assistants, Bookers, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles / Sydney
	1.2 Professional Context
The Model Agent operates at the heart of the talent ecosystem, performing a wide range of critical functions as documented in industry job descriptions :


* Talent Discovery & Development: "Discovering, developing and managing the careers of fashion models"
* Client Relationship Management: "Building meaningful relationships with both talent and clients"
* Booking & Negotiation: "Interpret creative briefs and match talent to client requirements, delivering commercially successful outcomes"
* Career Strategy: "Coordinate and manage the schedules, appointments and careers of represented models"
* Commercial Acumen: "Negotiate bookings, contracts and fees while ensuring the best interests of both clients and talent are represented"


The role is distinguished by its dual focus on talent welfare and commercial outcomes. As industry descriptions emphasize, successful agents must "maintain a strong understanding of both the Australian and international fashion markets" while being "passionate about helping others achieve long-term career success" . This requires a unique combination of business acumen, interpersonal skills, and deep industry knowledge.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Model Agent role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Model Agent is a pivotal figure in the fashion ecosystem, serving as the critical bridge between talent and opportunity. According to industry job descriptions, a Model Agent "plays a pivotal role in discovering, developing and managing the careers of fashion models" while "building meaningful relationships with both talent and clients." The role encompasses the full spectrum of talent management, from scouting and development to booking, negotiation, and career strategy. Model Agents are the architects of their talent's careers, guiding them from discovery to stardom while navigating the complex landscape of the fashion industry.


The profession operates within a dynamic and often challenging industry context. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) revealed that "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do." This environment demands that Model Agents not only possess exceptional commercial acumen and industry knowledge but also demonstrate genuine commitment to talent welfare and career development. As noted in industry descriptions, successful agents are "passionate about helping others achieve long-term career success" while navigating the "dynamic, fast-paced role requiring exceptional organizational skills, commercial acumen, creativity and a genuine passion for fashion."


The role encompasses various specializations, from scouting and development to international placement, brand management, and career transition. According to industry sources, the most successful agents are those who combine a keen eye for talent with strong business acumen and genuine care for their talent's wellbeing. The role is evolving with the digitization of the industry, with social media platforms like Instagram becoming essential for talent discovery and promotion.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 16 (Revised): TALENT SCOUT (Chasseur de Têtes / Talent Scout / Recruiter / Chercheur de Talents)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Talent Scout role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Talent Scout is a critical figure in the fashion ecosystem, serving as the primary discoverer of new talent across the industry. According to industry descriptions, a Talent Scout specializes in "identifying, evaluating, and recruiting talented individuals who can make significant contributions to creative projects." This role is the first step in the talent pipeline, requiring a unique combination of an "eagle eye for potential," deep industry knowledge, and exceptional interpersonal skills. Talent Scouts are the gatekeepers of the fashion industry, discovering the stars of tomorrow before they become known to the world.


The Talent Scout's function extends across the entire fashion value chain, from discovering emerging models and photographers to identifying creative directors, stylists, and other creative professionals. According to industry sources, "scouts are the 'talent pipeline' that keeps the fashion and entertainment industry vibrant and competitive." Without the work of talent scouts, "brands, agencies, and clients would have a hard time finding fresh faces and new perspectives." The role is essential for ensuring the continuous renewal of creative talent in the industry.


The role demands more than just a good eye. According to industry sources, effective scouts must "go beyond just looking at a potential model's physical appearance to assess their potential and marketability." This requires understanding current industry trends, client needs, and the broader fashion landscape. As such, successful scouts often have "experience in fashion or entertainment which is essential for success in scouting." The role is evolving with the digitization of talent discovery, with social media platforms like Instagram becoming essential scouting tools.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Chasseur de Têtes / Talent Scout / Recruiter / Chercheur de Talents / Scouteur
	English Job Title
	Talent Scout / Model Scout / Creative Talent Scout / Headhunter / Talent Recruiter
	ROME Code

	Convention Collective

	Supervises
	Junior Scouts, Scouting Assistants, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo / Sydney / Shanghai
	1.2 Professional Context
The Talent Scout operates at the very beginning of the talent lifecycle, serving as the primary gatekeeper for new talent entering the industry. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Talent Discovery: Identifying emerging talent through various channels including social media, in-person scouting, and referrals
* Talent Assessment: Evaluating potential talent, assessing their marketability, and identifying gaps in the current roster
* Talent Recruitment: Engaging potential talent, assessing interest, and initiating the representation process
* Market Intelligence: Staying informed of emerging fashion trends, industry developments, and new business opportunities
* Relationship Building: Developing and maintaining relationships with talent, clients, and key industry contacts
* Trend Forecasting: Anticipating what types of talent will be in demand


The role is distinguished by its focus on potential. As industry sources note, "identifying talent at the very beginning requires a particular eye and skill set that can't be taught." The best scouts have a demonstrated "ability to recognize potential before it's obvious to others." This intuitive ability, combined with industry knowledge and networking skills, makes talent scouting both an art and a science.


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements
	Model Scouting

	Keen aesthetic eye, industry knowledge
	Creative Scouting
	Identifying photographers, stylists, artists
	Industry network, creative knowledge
	Digital Scouting
	Discovering talent through social media
	Social media expertise, digital literacy
	International Scouting
	Finding talent in global markets
	International travel, cultural awareness
	Agency Scouting
	Scouting for specific agency rosters
	Understanding agency brand, client needs

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 17 (Revised): CASTING DIRECTOR (Directeur de Casting / Casting Director)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Casting Director role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Casting Director is a pivotal figure in the fashion ecosystem, responsible for selecting the talent that will bring creative visions to life across runway shows, advertising campaigns, editorial shoots, and brand activations. According to industry descriptions, the Casting Director "interprets creative briefs and selects the right talent to bring a creative vision to life, working across fashion shows, campaigns, and editorial projects." This role requires a unique combination of creative vision, deep industry knowledge, exceptional interpersonal skills, and the ability to understand and interpret the needs of designers, brands, and creative directors.


The Casting Director operates at the intersection of creativity and commerce, serving as the critical link between talent and opportunity. According to industry sources, successful casting directors possess "a deep understanding of the fashion market, current trends, and the unique requirements of each project." They must be able to "identify the right talent for each brief while building and maintaining strong relationships with models, agents, and clients." The role demands exceptional organizational skills, the ability to work under pressure, and a keen eye for potential.


The profession spans various specializations, from runway casting for fashion weeks to campaign casting for major brands, editorial casting for magazines, and commercial casting for advertising. According to industry research, the role has evolved significantly with the digitization of the industry, with social media and online platforms becoming essential tools for talent discovery and casting. The rise of diversity and inclusion has also transformed the role, with casting directors increasingly responsible for finding talent that represents a broader range of body types, ethnicities, and ages.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail

	English Job Title

	ROME Code

	1.2 Professional Context
The Casting Director operates at a critical junction in the fashion ecosystem, serving as the talent curator for creative projects. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Creative Interpretation: Understanding and interpreting creative briefs to select appropriate talent
* Talent Sourcing: Identifying and sourcing talent through agency networks, social media, and casting calls
* Talent Selection: Evaluating and selecting talent based on project requirements, marketability, and creative fit
* Client Communication: Managing relationships with clients, designers, and creative directors
* Agency Relationships: Building and maintaining strong relationships with model agencies and agents
* Trend Intelligence: Understanding current fashion trends and talent market dynamics


The role is distinguished by its dual focus on creative vision and commercial outcomes. As industry descriptions emphasize, successful casting directors must "understand the creative vision of each project" while "delivering commercially successful outcomes." This requires a unique combination of creative sensibility, business acumen, and deep industry knowledge.


The profession spans multiple specializations:

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 18 (Revised): RUNWAY MODEL (Mannequin de Défilé / Top Model / Catwalk Model)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Runway Model role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Runway Model represents the pinnacle of fashion modeling, walking the world's most prestigious catwalks to showcase designers' collections during fashion weeks and presentations. According to industry definitions, a runway model "works on the runway, showcasing designers' collections during fashion weeks and presentations," requiring "a specific walk, great posture, and the ability to embody the designer's vision." This role demands exceptional physical presence, technical skill, and the ability to transform on command to embody different designer aesthetics.


The profession operates within the "Image Economy"—career-building work that compoundable, where a single major moment can lead to the next, raising a model's profile and rate with each success. According to industry research, the runway modeling sector is characterized by intense competition, seasonal peaks, and significant income disparities. The COVID-19 pandemic fundamentally transformed the runway industry, accelerating the shift to digital presentations and creating new opportunities for virtual runway work.


The profession encompasses various tiers, from emerging and new faces to established and top models. According to industry sources, the most successful runway models are those who combine striking physical attributes with exceptional professionalism, versatility, and the ability to walk for diverse designers. The role is evolving with the growing emphasis on diversity and inclusion, with brands increasingly casting models of different body types, ethnicities, and ages. The rise of social media has also transformed the profession, with Instagram and TikTok becoming essential tools for personal branding and career advancement.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Mannequin de Défilé / Top Model / Mannequin Haute Couture
	English Job Title
	Runway Model / Catwalk Model / Fashion Model / Haute Couture Model
	ROME Code
	L1102 - Mannequinat et pose artistique
	Convention Collective
	Couture Parisienne - IDCC 303; Mannequins de Mode - IDCC 303
	Job Family
	Talent / Image Economy
	Reports To
	Casting Director / Show Director / Creative Director
	Supervises
	N/A
	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Tokyo / Shanghai / Dubai
	1.2 Professional Context
The Runway Model operates within the "Image Economy" of the fashion industry—work that builds reputation, visibility, and career momentum. According to industry definitions, this work is distinguished by its compoundable nature: "A single major editorial or campaign can lead to the next, raising a model's profile and rate with each success. This is career-building work."


The role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Physical Presence: Exceptional height, proportions, and posture
* Technical Skill: Mastery of runway walk, turning, and presentation
* Versatility: Ability to adapt to different designer aesthetics and show concepts
* Professionalism: Punctuality, reliability, and ability to work under pressure
* Brand Representation: Ability to embody brand values and creative vision
* Personal Branding: Building visibility and marketability through social media


The profession spans multiple tiers:


Tier
	Characteristics
	Key Requirements
	New Face
	0-2 years, building career
	Potential, trainability
	Emerging
	2-4 years, gaining visibility
	Developed walk, growing portfolio
	Established
	4-8 years, regular bookings
	Consistent work, client relationships
	Top Model
	8+ years, global recognition
	Major campaigns, fashion weeks
	Supermodel
	Elite level, cultural icon
	Global recognition, influence

The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Runway Model role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Runway Model represents the pinnacle of fashion modeling, walking the world's most prestigious catwalks to showcase designers' collections during fashion weeks and presentations. According to industry definitions, a runway model "works on the runway, showcasing designers' collections during fashion weeks and presentations," requiring "a specific walk, great posture, and the ability to embody the designer's vision." This role demands exceptional physical presence, technical skill, and the ability to transform on command to embody different designer aesthetics.


The profession operates within the "Image Economy"—career-building work that is compoundable, where a single major moment can lead to the next, raising a model's profile and rate with each success. According to industry research, the runway modeling sector is characterized by intense competition, seasonal peaks, and significant income disparities. The COVID-19 pandemic fundamentally transformed the runway industry, accelerating the shift to digital presentations and creating new opportunities for virtual runway work.


This document provides a detailed gender-based analysis of runway modeling requirements, followed by comprehensive profiles for each major modeling category, including entry requirements, pathways to success, value chains, command chains, key stakeholders, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 19 (Revised): EDITORIAL MODEL (Mannequin Éditorial / Modèle de Presse)
The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework
________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Editorial Model role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Editorial Model represents the creative and artistic pinnacle of fashion modeling, working with top photographers, stylists, and creative directors to produce high-fashion imagery for magazines, campaigns, and creative projects. According to industry definitions, an editorial model "poses for fashion magazines (like Vogue or Elle) and creative brand features," with "storytelling ability, emotion, and strong artistic vision" being essential. This role demands exceptional photogenic qualities, emotional range, and the ability to embody complex creative concepts.


The profession operates within the "Image Economy"—career-building work that is compoundable, where a single major editorial can lead to the next, raising a model's profile and rate with each success. According to industry research, editorial modeling is the most prestigious sector of the industry, with top editorial models achieving iconic status and commanding premium rates for campaigns and exclusive contracts. However, editorial work is also among the most competitive and challenging to secure.


This document provides a detailed gender-based analysis of editorial modeling requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major editorial modeling sub-category, from high-fashion editorial to beauty and lifestyle.


________________

* Peak Earnings (22-28): Highest income period

________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Commercial Model role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Commercial Model represents the accessible, relatable face of fashion and lifestyle brands, working across advertising campaigns, television commercials, print advertisements, digital content, and brand activations. According to industry definitions, a commercial model is "the face of a brand's advertising campaign for print, TV, or online," projecting "an accessible, relatable 'real person' look." This role demands exceptional natural presence, authenticity, and the ability to connect with diverse audiences while maintaining commercial viability.


The profession operates across both the "Image Economy" and the "Measure Economy," with commercial work providing more stable, consistent income while still building career momentum. According to industry research, the commercial modeling sector is the largest and most accessible segment of the modeling industry, with opportunities spanning a wide range of brands, products, and media. Unlike editorial or runway work, commercial modeling offers longer career spans, more diverse opportunities, and greater income stability.


This document provides a detailed gender-based analysis of commercial modeling requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major commercial modeling sub-category, from lifestyle and fitness to beauty and corporate.


________________
